And there have been times I’ve taken laxatives too, because I get scared the food is just going to stay in me. I know that probably sounds bad… I’m sorry.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intend to happen?\n\nPatient: I kept some food logs like you suggested—or, well, like I thought I should—and looking back, it’s more often than I wanted to admit. For many months now, nearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where I sort of lost control with eating… and then tried to make up for it somehow. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s what it looks like on paper.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know my doctor says I’m too thin, and I don’t want to argue with that, but if I get on the scale and it’s even up half a pound, I just… I feel panicky. Ashamed, really. Like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away. I know that probably doesn’t sound sensible.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, doctor. I mean, I check sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve kept track well enough to say there’s been a significant change. It may go up and down a bit, but I’d hate to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do. I don’t like saying it that way, because I know you’ve told me I’m too thin, and I’m not trying to be difficult. But if the scale goes up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky, ashamed feeling, like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly. I suppose other people might see me differently than I see myself, but I don’t really know. I just know what I notice when I look at myself, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself, do you see yourself as being the same weight as what others tell you you are?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it matches, doctor. People tell me one thing, and I try to take that in, but when I look in the mirror it doesn’t quite feel that way to me. I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for… I just have trouble seeing it the way they describe it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I know my doctor says I’m too thin, and I don’t want to argue with that, but if I get on the scale and it’s even up half a pound, I just… I feel panicky. Ashamed, really. Like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away. I know that probably doesn’t sound sensible.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, doctor. I mean, I check sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve kept track well enough to say there’s been a significant change. It may go up and down a bit, but I’d hate to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do. I don’t like saying it that way, because I know you’ve told me I’m too thin, and I’m not trying to be difficult. But if the scale goes up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky, ashamed feeling, like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly. I suppose other people might see me differently than I see myself, but I don’t really know. I just know what I notice when I look at myself, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself, do you see yourself as being the same weight as what others tell you you are?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it matches, doctor. People tell me one thing, and I try to take that in, but when I look in the mirror it doesn’t quite feel that way to me. I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for… I just have trouble seeing it the way they describe it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect what you do from day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I like to admit. I try not to make a fuss about it, but I’ve stopped going to the garden group as much, and I avoid family meals sometimes. I’m either planning around the food, or feeling ashamed afterward, or worrying someone’s going to notice what I’m doing. I know that probably sounds silly at my age, but… yes, it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see a fat person?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. “Fat person” sounds harsh, doesn’t it? I just… when I look, I see places that seem too big, or not right. Other people might not call it that, but it’s hard for me not to notice. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time where you lost a significant amount of weight, only to regain it later?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I could say it was “significant,” doctor. My weight has gone up and down some, but I haven’t really kept track in a proper way. I suppose there may have been times I lost a bit and then it came back, but… I don’t know if it would count as what you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away. I know that probably doesn’t sound sensible.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, doctor. I mean, I check sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve kept track well enough to say there’s been a significant change. It may go up and down a bit, but I’d hate to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do. I don’t like saying it that way, because I know you’ve told me I’m too thin, and I’m not trying to be difficult. But if the scale goes up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky, ashamed feeling, like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly. I suppose other people might see me differently than I see myself, but I don’t really know. I just know what I notice when I look at myself, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself, do you see yourself as being the same weight as what others tell you you are?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it matches, doctor. People tell me one thing, and I try to take that in, but when I look in the mirror it doesn’t quite feel that way to me. I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for… I just have trouble seeing it the way they describe it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect what you do from day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I like to admit. I try not to make a fuss about it, but I’ve stopped going to the garden group as much, and I avoid family meals sometimes. I’m either planning around the food, or feeling ashamed afterward, or worrying someone’s going to notice what I’m doing. I know that probably sounds silly at my age, but… yes, it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see a fat person?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. “Fat person” sounds harsh, doesn’t it? I just… when I look, I see places that seem too big, or not right. Other people might not call it that, but it’s hard for me not to notice. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time where you lost a significant amount of weight, only to regain it later?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I could say it was “significant,” doctor. My weight has gone up and down some, but I haven’t really kept track in a proper way. I suppose there may have been times I lost a bit and then it came back, but… I don’t know if it would count as what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight dominate your life most of the time?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t want to say it “dominates” exactly… that sounds a bit dramatic. But, yes, it’s on my mind a lot. Even when you’ve told me I’m too thin, if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this panicky, ashamed feeling, like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away. I know that may not sound reasonable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because I’m having some trouble with my eating. It’s a bit embarrassing to say. Once I start, it feels like my hands just keep going back for more, even when I’m telling myself, “Tom, stop this right now.” That’s the part that scares me most, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I don’t know if that’s the right word. I do feel bad afterward sometimes, like I wish I’d handled it better, but… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to get rid of the calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… yes, sometimes. I’m not proud of it. If I feel like I’ve eaten too much, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. And there have been times I’ve taken laxatives too, because I get scared the food is just going to stay in me. I know that probably sounds bad… I’m sorry.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intend to happen?\n\nPatient: I kept some food logs like you suggested—or, well, like I thought I should—and looking back, it’s more often than I wanted to admit. For many months now, nearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where I sort of lost control with eating… and then tried to make up for it somehow. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s what it looks like on paper.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know my doctor says I’m too thin, and I don’t want to argue with that, but if I get on the scale and it’s even up half a pound, I just… I feel panicky. Ashamed, really. Like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away. I know that probably doesn’t sound sensible.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, doctor. I mean, I check sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve kept track well enough to say there’s been a significant change. It may go up and down a bit, but I’d hate to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do. I don’t like saying it that way, because I know you’ve told me I’m too thin, and I’m not trying to be difficult. But if the scale goes up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky, ashamed feeling, like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly. I suppose other people might see me differently than I see myself, but I don’t really know. I just know what I notice when I look at myself, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself, do you see yourself as being the same weight as what others tell you you are?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it matches, doctor. People tell me one thing, and I try to take that in, but when I look in the mirror it doesn’t quite feel that way to me. I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for… I just have trouble seeing it the way they describe it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect what you do from day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I like to admit. I try not to make a fuss about it, but I’ve stopped going to the garden group as much, and I avoid family meals sometimes. I’m either planning around the food, or feeling ashamed afterward, or worrying someone’s going to notice what I’m doing. I know that probably sounds silly at my age, but… yes, it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see a fat person?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. “Fat person” sounds harsh, doesn’t it? I just… when I look, I see places that seem too big, or not right. Other people might not call it that, but it’s hard for me not to notice. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time where you lost a significant amount of weight, only to regain it later?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I could say it was “significant,” doctor. My weight has gone up and down some, but I haven’t really kept track in a proper way. I suppose there may have been times I lost a bit and then it came back, but… I don’t know if it would count as what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight dominate your life most of the time?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t want to say it “dominates” exactly… that sounds a bit dramatic. But, yes, it’s on my mind a lot. Even when you’ve told me I’m too thin, if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this panicky, ashamed feeling, like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away. I know that may not sound reasonable.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat less during the day to compensate for the episodes of eating more than you intend to?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… I suppose I do. I don’t mean to make a big thing of it, but if I’ve had one of those bad eating spells, the next day I’ll often tell myself I don’t need breakfast, and then lunch sort of gets skipped too. And I’ll try to keep busy—walking laps, doing yard chores—maybe longer than I should, even when my knees and back are complaining. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of gaining weight is something that you think about almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that would be fair to say. I don’t like admitting it, but it’s there most days. Even with my doctor telling me I’m too thin, if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this panicky, ashamed feeling—like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it. I know that probably sounds foolish.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Once I start, it feels like my hands just keep going back for more, even when I’m telling myself, “Tom, stop this right now.” That’s the part that scares me most, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. I don’t know if that’s the right word. I do feel bad afterward sometimes, like I wish I’d handled it better, but… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to get rid of the calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… yes, sometimes. I’m not proud of it. If I feel like I’ve eaten too much, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. And there have been times I’ve taken laxatives too, because I get scared the food is just going to stay in me. I know that probably sounds bad… I’m sorry.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intend to happen?\n\nPatient: I kept some food logs like you suggested—or, well, like I thought I should—and looking back, it’s more often than I wanted to admit. For many months now, nearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where I sort of lost control with eating… and then tried to make up for it somehow. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s what it looks like on paper.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know my doctor says I’m too thin, and I don’t want to argue with that, but if I get on the scale and it’s even up half a pound, I just… I feel panicky. Ashamed, really. Like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away. I know that probably doesn’t sound sensible.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, doctor. I mean, I check sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve kept track well enough to say there’s been a significant change. It may go up and down a bit, but I’d hate to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do. I don’t like saying it that way, because I know you’ve told me I’m too thin, and I’m not trying to be difficult. I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for… I just have trouble seeing it the way they describe it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect what you do from day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I like to admit. I try not to make a fuss about it, but I’ve stopped going to the garden group as much, and I avoid family meals sometimes. Even with my doctor telling me I’m too thin, if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this panicky, ashamed feeling—like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it. I know that probably sounds foolish.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I know how to answer that exactly, doctor. I mean… I look at the scale and then I look in the mirror, and I don’t always know if they match. Maybe I’m not the best judge of it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you look in the mirror, do you see your body as it actually is?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 78 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. I don’t know if that’s the right word. I do feel bad afterward sometimes, like I wish I’d handled it better, but… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to get rid of the calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… yes, sometimes. I’m not proud of it. If I feel like I’ve eaten too much, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. And there have been times I’ve taken laxatives too, because I get scared the food is just going to stay in me. I know that probably sounds bad… I’m sorry.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intend to happen?\n\nPatient: I kept some food logs like you suggested—or, well, like I thought I should—and looking back, it’s more often than I wanted to admit. For many months now, nearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where I sort of lost control with eating… and then tried to make up for it somehow. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s what it looks like on paper.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know my doctor says I’m too thin, and I don’t want to argue with that, but if I get on the scale and it’s even up half a pound, I just… I feel panicky. Ashamed, really. Like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away. I know that probably doesn’t sound sensible.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, doctor. I mean, I check sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve kept track well enough to say there’s been a significant change. It may go up and down a bit, but I’d hate to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do. I don’t like saying it that way, because I know you’ve told me I’m too thin, and I’m not trying to be difficult. But if the scale goes up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky, ashamed feeling, like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly. I suppose other people might see me differently than I see myself, but I don’t really know. I just know what I notice when I look at myself, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself, do you see yourself as being the same weight as what others tell you you are?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it matches, doctor. People tell me one thing, and I try to take that in, but when I look in the mirror it doesn’t quite feel that way to me. I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for… I just have trouble seeing it the way they describe it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect what you do from day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I like to admit. I try not to make a fuss about it, but I’ve stopped going to the garden group as much, and I avoid family meals sometimes. I’m either planning around the food, or feeling ashamed afterward, or worrying someone’s going to notice what I’m doing. I know that probably sounds silly at my age, but… yes, it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see a fat person?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. “Fat person” sounds harsh, doesn’t it? I know that probably sounds bad… I’m sorry.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intend to happen?\n\nPatient: I kept some food logs like you suggested—or, well, like I thought I should—and looking back, it’s more often than I wanted to admit. For many months now, nearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where I sort of lost control with eating… and then tried to make up for it somehow. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s what it looks like on paper.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know my doctor says I’m too thin, and I don’t want to argue with that, but if I get on the scale and it’s even up half a pound, I just… I feel panicky. Ashamed, really. Like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away. I know that probably doesn’t sound sensible.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, doctor. I mean, I check sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve kept track well enough to say there’s been a significant change. It may go up and down a bit, but I’d hate to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do. I don’t like saying it that way, because I know you’ve told me I’m too thin, and I’m not trying to be difficult. But if the scale goes up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky, ashamed feeling, like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly. I suppose other people might see me differently than I see myself, but I don’t really know. I just know what I notice when I look at myself, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself, do you see yourself as being the same weight as what others tell you you are?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it matches, doctor. People tell me one thing, and I try to take that in, but when I look in the mirror it doesn’t quite feel that way to me. I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for… I just have trouble seeing it the way they describe it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect what you do from day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I like to admit. I try not to make a fuss about it, but I’ve stopped going to the garden group as much, and I avoid family meals sometimes. I’m either planning around the food, or feeling ashamed afterward, or worrying someone’s going to notice what I’m doing. I know that probably sounds silly at my age, but… yes, it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see a fat person?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. “Fat person” sounds harsh, doesn’t it? Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time where you lost a significant amount of weight, only to regain it later?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I could say it was “significant,” doctor. Once I start, it feels like my hands just keep going back for more, even when I’m telling myself, “Tom, stop this right now.” That’s the part that scares me most, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to get rid of the calories after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… yes, sometimes. I’m not proud of it. If I feel like I’ve eaten too much, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. And there have been times I’ve taken laxatives too, because I get scared the food is just going to stay in me. I know that probably sounds bad… I’m sorry.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intend to happen?\n\nPatient: I kept some food logs like you suggested—or, well, like I thought I should—and looking back, it’s more often than I wanted to admit. For many months now, nearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where I sort of lost control with eating… and then tried to make up for it somehow. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s what it looks like on paper.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know my doctor says I’m too thin, and I don’t want to argue with that, but if I get on the scale and it’s even up half a pound, I just… I feel panicky. Ashamed, really. Like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away. I know that probably doesn’t sound sensible.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, doctor. I mean, I check sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve kept track well enough to say there’s been a significant change. It may go up and down a bit, but I’d hate to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do. I don’t like saying it that way, because I know you’ve told me I’m too thin, and I’m not trying to be difficult. But if the scale goes up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky, ashamed feeling, like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly. I suppose other people might see me differently than I see myself, but I don’t really know. I just know what I notice when I look at myself, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself, do you see yourself as being the same weight as what others tell you you are?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it matches, doctor. People tell me one thing, and I try to take that in, but when I look in the mirror it doesn’t quite feel that way to me. I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for… I just have trouble seeing it the way they describe it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect what you do from day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I like to admit. I try not to make a fuss about it, but I’ve stopped going to the garden group as much, and I avoid family meals sometimes. I’m either planning around the food, or feeling ashamed afterward, or worrying someone’s going to notice what I’m doing. I know that probably sounds silly at my age, but… yes, it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see a fat person?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. “Fat person” sounds harsh, doesn’t it? I just… when I look, I see places that seem too big, or not right. Other people might not call it that, but it’s hard for me not to notice. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time where you lost a significant amount of weight, only to regain it later?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I could say it was “significant,” doctor. My weight has gone up and down some, but I haven’t really kept track in a proper way. I suppose there may have been times I lost a bit and then it came back, but… I don’t know if it would count as what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight dominate your life most of the time?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t want to say it “dominates” exactly… that sounds a bit dramatic. But, yes, it’s on my mind a lot. Even when you’ve told me I’m too thin, if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this panicky, ashamed feeling, like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away. I know that may not sound reasonable.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat less during the day to compensate for the episodes of eating more than you intend to?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… I suppose I do. I don’t mean to make a big thing of it, but if I’ve had one of those bad eating spells, the next day I’ll often tell myself I don’t need breakfast, and then lunch sort of gets skipped too. And I’ll try to keep busy—walking laps, doing yard chores—maybe longer than I should, even when my knees and back are complaining. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of gaining weight is something that you think about almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that would be fair to say. I don’t like admitting it, but it’s there most days. Even with my doctor telling me I’m too thin, if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this panicky, ashamed feeling—like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it. I know that probably sounds foolish.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I know how to answer that exactly, doctor. I mean… I look at the scale and then I look in the mirror, and I don’t always know if they match. Maybe I’m not the best judge of it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see your body as it actually is?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not completely sure, doctor. Some days I think I’m seeing myself plainly, and other days I don’t know if my eyes are playing tricks on me a bit. I don’t want to exaggerate it or give you the wrong answer. I just… I’m not sure I trust what I see all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be considered underweight by others if they saw you?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, probably. I mean, my doctor has said I’m too thin, so I know other people might see it that way. But it’s hard for me to feel that way myself. If the scale goes up even half a pound, I get this panicky, ashamed feeling, like I’ve let myself go and need to do something about it right away. I know that may not sound sensible.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal for your height?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it is, doctor… I know you’ve told me I’m too thin, so I’m trying to take that in. But honestly, it doesn’t feel normal to me in my head. If the scale is even up half a pound, I get this panic and shame, like I’ve let myself go and need to fix it straightaway. I know that probably sounds a bit unreasonable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F50.0", "F50.2"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

If I feel like I’ve eaten too much, I’ll go to the bathroom and make myself bring it back up. And there have been times I’ve taken laxatives too, because I get scared the food is just going to stay in me. I know that probably sounds bad… I’m sorry.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you intend to happen?\n\nPatient: I kept some food logs like you suggested—or, well, like I thought I should—and looking back, it’s more often than I wanted to admit. For many months now, nearly every week there’s been at least one afternoon where I sort of lost control with eating… and then tried to make up for it somehow. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s what it looks like on paper.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about gaining weight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I know my doctor says I’m too thin, and I don’t want to argue with that, but if I get on the scale and it’s even up half a pound, I just… I feel panicky. Ashamed, really. Like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away. I know that probably doesn’t sound sensible.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure, doctor. I mean, I check sometimes, but I don’t know that I’ve kept track well enough to say there’s been a significant change. It may go up and down a bit, but I’d hate to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a fear of becoming fat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I do. I don’t like saying it that way, because I know you’ve told me I’m too thin, and I’m not trying to be difficult. But if the scale goes up even half a pound, I get this awful panicky, ashamed feeling, like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly. I suppose other people might see me differently than I see myself, but I don’t really know. I just know what I notice when I look at myself, if that makes sense. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you look at yourself, do you see yourself as being the same weight as what others tell you you are?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say it matches, doctor. People tell me one thing, and I try to take that in, but when I look in the mirror it doesn’t quite feel that way to me. I don’t know if that’s the answer you’re looking for… I just have trouble seeing it the way they describe it.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight affect what you do from day to day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it does, more than I like to admit. I try not to make a fuss about it, but I’ve stopped going to the garden group as much, and I avoid family meals sometimes. I’m either planning around the food, or feeling ashamed afterward, or worrying someone’s going to notice what I’m doing. I know that probably sounds silly at my age, but… yes, it does get in the way.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see a fat person?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d put it that strongly. “Fat person” sounds harsh, doesn’t it? I just… when I look, I see places that seem too big, or not right. Other people might not call it that, but it’s hard for me not to notice. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods of time where you lost a significant amount of weight, only to regain it later?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I could say it was “significant,” doctor. My weight has gone up and down some, but I haven’t really kept track in a proper way. I suppose there may have been times I lost a bit and then it came back, but… I don’t know if it would count as what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of gaining weight dominate your life most of the time?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t want to say it “dominates” exactly… that sounds a bit dramatic. But, yes, it’s on my mind a lot. Even when you’ve told me I’m too thin, if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this panicky, ashamed feeling, like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it right away. I know that may not sound reasonable.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat less during the day to compensate for the episodes of eating more than you intend to?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… I suppose I do. I don’t mean to make a big thing of it, but if I’ve had one of those bad eating spells, the next day I’ll often tell myself I don’t need breakfast, and then lunch sort of gets skipped too. And I’ll try to keep busy—walking laps, doing yard chores—maybe longer than I should, even when my knees and back are complaining. I know that probably isn’t the best way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of gaining weight is something that you think about almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that would be fair to say. I don’t like admitting it, but it’s there most days. Even with my doctor telling me I’m too thin, if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this panicky, ashamed feeling—like I’ve let myself go and I need to fix it. I know that probably sounds foolish.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I know how to answer that exactly, doctor. I mean… I look at the scale and then I look in the mirror, and I don’t always know if they match. Maybe I’m not the best judge of it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see your body as it actually is?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not completely sure, doctor. Some days I think I’m seeing myself plainly, and other days I don’t know if my eyes are playing tricks on me a bit. I don’t want to exaggerate it or give you the wrong answer. I just… I’m not sure I trust what I see all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be considered underweight by others if they saw you?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, probably. I mean, my doctor has said I’m too thin, so I know other people might see it that way. But it’s hard for me to feel that way myself. If the scale goes up even half a pound, I get this panicky, ashamed feeling, like I’ve let myself go and need to do something about it right away. I know that may not sound sensible.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal for your height?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say it is, doctor… I know you’ve told me I’m too thin, so I’m trying to take that in. But honestly, it doesn’t feel normal to me in my head. If the scale is even up half a pound, I get this panic and shame, like I’ve let myself go and need to fix it straightaway. I know that probably sounds a bit unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your fear of gaining weight controls most of your daily thoughts and actions?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t like to admit it that strongly, doctor, but yes, I suppose it does more than I’d want. Even when you’ve said I’m too thin, if the scale is up just half a pound I feel panicky and ashamed, like I’ve let myself go. Then I feel I have to fix it straight away. I know that probably isn’t the right way to think about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
